## 2.4.3. Discussion on clinical efficacy

## Design and conduct of clinical studies

This application is based upon the interim PFS results of the pivotal phase 3 study MajesTEC-9 (64007957MMY3006), a randomised (1:1), open-label, multi-centre, phase 3 study that evaluated the efficacy and safety of teclistamab versus pomalidomide, bortezomib and dexamethasone (PVd) or carfilzomib and dexamethasone (Kd) in subjects with relapsed or refractory multiple myeloma. Randomisation was stratified based on investigator's choice of PVd or Kd, stage of disease at screening per ISS (I vs II/III), number of prior lines of therapy, and anti-CD38 monoclonal antibody refractory status . A total of 593 participants were randomised to teclistamab: (N=296) or PVd /Kd (N=297), 93 to PVd and 204 to Kd.

The stratification factors have prognostic implications in this setting and are deemed clinically appropriate.

\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## Study participants

Inclusion and exclusion criteria are overall acceptable. Key inclusion criteria were relapsed or refractory multiple myeloma according to IMWG criteria with measurable disease.

Participants with CNS/meningeal involvement by MM or other severe neurologic conditions were excluded from trial participation. Patients with low cardiac ejection fraction or patients with rarer forms of plasma cell neoplasms, such as plasma cell leukaemia, primary systemic light-chain amyloidosis, Waldenstrom macroglobulinemia, or POEMS syndrome were also excluded. As requested, this information has been added to section 5.1 of the SmPC.

Participants must have had to receive a minimum of 2 consecutive cycles of an anti-CD38 monoclonal in any prior line and 2 consecutive cycles of lenalidomide in any prior line. This rather arbitrary requirement is not totally in line with the current clinical practice. However, it led to enrolment of a relevant patient population.

While the Kd-regimen may not provide the best standard of care for all patients, it is still a valid second or later line option, as carfilzomib is approved in second or later line.

For the other comparator used in the study, PVd, it should be noted that in current treatment practice bortezomib is positioned as part of the first line treatment in different combinations.

Reintroducing it later on, might not be optimal in a considerable number of patients due to cumulative toxicity (i.e. neuropathy). This is possibly why a lower number of patients were treated with PVd (93) compared to Kd (204).

## Dose and administration regimen

Teclistamab was administered SC on 28-day cycles using a weight-based dosing schedule consisting of 2 step-up doses (0.06 and 0.3 mg/kg) on Day 1 and 3, followed by a treatment dosing schedule of 1.5 mg/kg weekly in the first 2 cycles starting from Day 8, then 3 mg/kg Q2W in Cycles 3 through 6, and then 3 mg/kg Q4W in Cycle 7 and onwards.

Comparator treatment regimens were administered according to the SmPC of Imnovid, Velcade and Kyprolis. Most of the data to support the dosing of carfilzomib and dexamethasone (Kd) have used twice weekly dosing of carfilzomib (20/56 mg/m² twice weekly), which is the EU authorised dosing. Once weekly dosing for Kd (20/70 mg/m²), which is an option in US, but not in EU was also allowed. In the comparator group with Kd, once weekly dosing was used in 56.9% and twice weekly in 43.1% of the participants. However, as there was no difference between the HRs of Kd with the two carfilzomib dosing regimens this is not considered to impact the reported results.

## Objectives/endpoints

The primary objective was to determine whether teclistamab will significantly improve PFS compared with investigator's choice of PVd /Kd in participants who have received 1 to 3 prior lines of therapy, including a anti-CD38monoclonal antibody and lenalidomide.

The primary endpoint was PFS by IRC which is considered acceptable. The other endpoints include response rates (CR or better, VGPR or better and OR), OS, HRQoL, PFS2, duration of response, time to next treatment, among others. These endpoints are established ones in MM, and as such appropriate.

\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## Statistical methods

In this open-label trial, key elements of the statistical analyses were predefined in the original protocol and in the initial SAP, released two months after screening the first participant. As such the analyses are considered predefined unless otherwise noted.

The statistical analysis of the primary endpoint, PFS based in IRC assessment, was conventional applying stratified log-rank test and stratified Cox proportional hazards model stratified by the randomisation strata. The fact that participants were censored at the last disease assessment prior to starting subsequent antimyeloma treatment, if the latter was started prior to disease progression, or prior to two or more consecutive missed disease assessment - neither being supported by the relevant EMA guideline - is not considered an issue since there were relatively few such instances and the results of the sensitivity analyses without such censoring were consistent.

The hierarchical hypothesis testing across the primary and key secondary endpoints along with the group-sequential testing across the interim and final analyses are adequate methods of controlling type 1 error to the acceptable level of 5% (2-sided).

In addition to the general concerns about bias related to PROs in open-label trials, there are further concerns about collection and statistical analysis of these data. The different cycle lengths mean assessment times are not comparable between and within treatment arms, but nevertheless data were summarised by treatment cycle.

In the absence of submitted protocols before Amendment 4, it is not known whether PROs were to be collected from all randomised participants irrespective of whether they were using the study treatment initiated at baseline and irrespective of starting another antimyeloma therapy. Furthermore, frequency of assessment was changed to Q16W after disease progression. Given the different enrolment times, some participants are expected to be censored administratively but information on how administrative censoring impacted PRO data is not included in the submission. Furthermore, applying a mixed model for repeated measures on a data set consisting of only ontreatment data, implies an estimand corresponding to average QoL in the hypothetical scenario where participants' disease did not progress and no-one died and is, as such, not relevant in the RRMM setting.

PROs were analysed also considering time to event, e.g. time to worsening in MySIm-Q Total Symptom Subscale, a predefined key secondary endpoint. In this analysis, a defined level (MID) of worsening (with no subsequent improvement below the MID threshold at later assessments) or death due to disease progression were counted as events. Data were censored in case of death due to a reason other than progressive disease or when subsequent anti-myeloma therapy was started. The definitions of events, censoring rules, and determination of whether event was 'sustained' are considered inconsistent. The analysis assumes that censoring due to subsequent anti-myeloma therapy (which is predominant in the data) is uninformative about the risk of symptom worsening. This assumption is questionable. The question addressed by this analysis is also ambiguous since, on the one hand, it appears to evaluate symptom worsening independent from disease progression (by assuming that censoring due to other anti-myeloma treatments and considerably more sparse PRO assessment schedule are not issues) while, at the same time, considering death due to disease progression as events. Also considering the questionable concept of \"sustained\" symptom worsening, the analysis of time to worsening in MySIm-Q Total Symptom Subscale and other similar analyses included in the CSR are not credible.

\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## Study conduct

Protocol amendments have been appropriately justified and are not believed to have affected trial integrity.

Major protocol deviations were few and mostly balanced between study arms. Protocol deviations are considered not to have had major impact on trial conduct or study results.

## Baseline characteristics

The demographic and clinical characteristics of the participants at baseline were generally balanced in the two groups and reflect a rather young and fit patient population with median age of 70 (range: 34 to 86) years. Only roughly one third of the patients were less than 65 years, and therefore the enrolled patients represent an average (older) MM patient in first or later relapse.

Most of the participants have received 2 or more lines of prior therapies, with a median of 2 lines in both groups. Approximately 22% of the participants had received only one previous line of treatment. Thus, second line population was not well represented in the trial. Results in this subgroup were very consistent with those treated in third or later line and therefore it is expected that the treatment effect is not impacted by the number of prior treatments.

## Efficacy data and additional analyses

The study met its primary endpoint and demonstrated a statistically significant improvement in PFS in the teclistamab group compared with the PVd/Kd group. With a median follow-up of 17.3 months in the ITT population, median PFS was not reached in the teclistamab group and was 8.2 months (95% CI: 7.03, 9.36) in the comparator group [HR 0.29 (95% CI: 0.23, 0.38), p &lt;0.0001)]. Results of all sensitivity analyses were consistent with the primary PFS analysis.

The PFS result of the comparator group, mainly derived by Kd, is considerably shorter than 18.7 months observed in the pivotal study (ENDEAVOR, Dimopoulos et.al, Lancet Oncol 2017 ), which was the basis of the approval of Kd in the second line treatment in RRMM. There are several reasons for the somewhat unexpectedly short PFS, including higher number of prior treatment with bortezomib and lenalidomide, the fact that most patient were refractory to lenalidomide and daratumumab and the observation that only 22% of the participants had received only one previous line of treatment in MajesTEC-9. Despite the suboptimal efficacy in the comparator group, the efficacy of teclistamab monotherapy with the observed PFS result is nevertheless meaningful in the studied patient population taking into account the fact that most patients were refractory to both lenalidomide and daratumumab.

Importantly, the study also showed an OS benefit of teclistamab with HR of 0.60 (95% CI: 0.43, 0.83); p&lt;0.0020. Positive treatment effect (HR&lt;1) was demonstrated in all subgroups evaluated except the standard-risk cytogenetic and bone marrow plasma cells &gt;30% to &lt;60% subgroups. It is agreed with the MAH that the relatively short duration of follow-up for OS (standard-risk cytogenetic) and reduced size of the subgroup for bone marrow plasma cells &gt;30% to &lt;60% might be the reasons behind these unexpected findings.

While both PFS and OS are formally positive efficacy endpoints (results from preplanned interim analysis), follow up for both PFS and OS is currently limited (median follow-up of 17.3 months). Longer OS follow-up is also of interest considering the observation in patients with standard-risk cytogenetic, and therefore the CHMP recommended that PFS and OS updates from this study be submitted when available.

\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

All key and other secondary endpoints, including CR or better, OR, duration of response, rate, were in line with the primary endpoint favouring teclistamab.

Although classified as one of the key secondary endpoints included in the statistical hierarchy, the interpretability of the PRO data is hampered by the open-label study design and the strategy for PRO assessments as discussed above.

## 2.4.4. Conclusions on the clinical efficacy

The use of teclistamab monotherapy translates into a significant delay in the progression of the disease in the targeted patient population, i.e. patients with multiple myeloma who have received at least one prior line of therapy. This improvement is considered clinically relevant and is supported by key secondary endpoints, including OS and response rates (especially CR or better).

## 2.5. Clinical safety

## Introduction

To support this extension of indication application, the MAH has provided safety data from291 participants in the teclistamab group and 283 participants in the control group available at the time of the protocol-specified interim analysis of PFS (CCO date 13 October 2025) from Study 64007957MMY3006 (hereafter referred to as MajesTEC-9.

## Patient exposure

## Disposition of patients

In the Safety Analysis Set, 101/291 participants (34.7%) in the teclistamab group and 215/283 participants (76.0%) in the PVd/Kd group discontinued study treatment. The most common reason for discontinuation of study treatment in each treatment group was progressive disease (teclistamab group 20.6%, control group 54.1%). In the ITT Analysis Set, 61/296 participants (20.6%) in the teclistamab group and 97/297 participants (32.7%) in the PVd/Kd group discontinued study participation, most commonly due to death (teclistamab: 20.3%; control: 29.3%).

## Duration of follow-up

As of the CCO, the overall median duration of follow-up was 17.3 months (range: 0.1 to 29.0, for tec 17.25 months, for PVd/Kd 17.38 months).

## Exposure to treatment

The median duration of treatment was longer in the teclistamab group (13.1 months [range: 0.10 to 28.5]) than the PVd/Kd group (7.0 months [range: 0.03 to 27.9]). At the time of CCO, 52.9% of participants in the teclistamab group reached at least 12 months on study treatment compared with 24.7% in the PVd/Kd group, and 27.1% of participants in the teclistamab group reached at least 18 months on study treatment compared with 9.9% in the PVd/Kd group.

The overall median relative dose intensity of teclistamab was 98.3%. The median relative dose intensity of teclistamab remained high across the treatment cycle intervals (99.1% for Cycle 1, 99.6% for Cycle 2, 99.7% for Cycles 3-6, and 99.9% for Cycle 7 and onwards).

\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Ninety-eight participants (33.7%) receiving teclistamab switched to Q4W prior to Cycle 7 due to confirmed VGPR or better.

## Cycle delays, dose modifications, and dose reductions

Cycle delays were reported in 66.3% of participants in the teclistamab group. An AE was the most frequently reported reason for cycle delays. Dose modifications (dose interruptions [i.e., dose delays and skips] or reductions and schedule changes) for teclistamab occurred in 55.7% of participants. Dose delays and dose skips for teclistamab due to TEAE occurred in 16.8% and 42.3% of participants, respectively. Teclistamab dose reduction from 3 mg/kg to 1.5 mg/kg to manage toxicities was allowed per protocol.

## Repeat step-up and treatment dosing

There were 66 participants (22.7%) in the teclistamab group with at least 1 repeat step-up dosing schedule at any time during the study. The majority of these 66 participants received repeat dosing after the first treatment dose (56 participants [84.8%]). Of these 56 participants, 48 (85.7%) received all or part of the repeat step-up dosing schedule in an outpatient setting.

## Concomitant medications

## Prophylactic antimicrobial medications

Prophylactic antiviral medications were prescribed for 273 participants (93.8%) in the teclistamab group and 259 participants (91.5%) in the PVd/Kd group, most commonly aciclovir (teclistamab: 167 participants [57.4%]; PVd/Kd: 161 participants [56.9%]).

Prophylactic antibacterial medications were prescribed for 245 participants (84.2%) in the teclistamab group and 184 participants (65.0%) in the PVd/Kd group, most commonly sulfamethoxazole/trimethoprim (teclistamab: 215 participants [73.9%]; PVd/Kd: 159 participants [56.2%].

Prophylactic antifungal medications were prescribed for 15 participants (5.2%) in the teclistamab group and 16 participants (5.7%) in the PVd/Kd group, most commonly fluconazole (teclistamab: 11 participants [3.8%]; PVd/Kd: 14 participants [4.9%]).

## Systemic steroid use

Per protocol, participants in the teclistamab arm were not to receive dexamethasone as pretreatment medication after Cycle 1 Day 5, while participants in the PVd/Kd group received dexamethasone as part of their treatment regimen. Accordingly, the overall use of steroids during the study was lower in the teclistamab arm than in the PVd/Kd group, as indicated by the lower median total steroid dose received by participants in the teclistamab group (68.0 mg) vs the control group (660.0 mg).

## Immunoglobulin use

A total of 275 participants (94.5%) in the teclistamab arm and 151 participants (53.4%) in the PVd/Kd group received at least 1 dose of IV or SC immunoglobulin treatment as a concomitant medication.

## Growth factors use

Hematologic growth factors were utilised for 134 participants (46.0%) in the teclistamab arm and 57 participants (20.1%) in the PVd/Kd group.

In the teclistamab arm, grade 1-4 TEAEs were reported with similar frequencies in patients that had received one, two or three prior lines (data not shown). After one prior line there were no grade 5 TEAEs in patients treated with teclistamab, while nine patients after two prior lines experienced a grade 5 TEAE (7%) and ten patients after three prior lines (10%). A similarly increasing trend in SAEs is seen with the burden from prior therapies (one prior line, 41.3%, two prior lines, 57%, and three prior lines, 66%). Also, discontinuation of tec was most frequent in patients with three prior lines (12%), similar to patients with two prior lines (11.7%) and lowest after one prior line (6.3%).

144 patients in the teclistamab arm had a history of HSCT (autologous, allogeneic, once or twice) while 147 patients had not. No clear differences or trends in grade 1-5 TEAEs can be seen in the two populations. Also, the frequencies of TEAEs leading to death, SAEs and TEAEs leading to discontinuation are similar.

## Adverse Drug Reactions

Adverse drug reactions are AEs considered to be reasonably associated with the use of teclistamab based on a comprehensive assessment of available AE information. The sponsor's medical experts evaluated data using the definition of ADRs from the International Council for Harmonisation of Technical Requirements for Pharmaceuticals for Human Use guideline entitled, \"ICH E6(R3): Guideline for Good Clinical Practice.\" The determination of ADRs is based on the combined

\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

assessment of incidence, overall trends, biological plausibility, medical judgment, and individual case review, as necessary.

ADR assessment for MajesTEC-9 discussed in this section was based on all TEAEs and laboratory abnormalities reported in the 291 participants in the Safety Analysis Set in the teclistamab group. The ADR analysis of TEAEs was conducted in a stepwise manner according to the following criteria:

- TEAEs as individual preferred terms from MajesTEC-9 were first analyzed based on the below:

- TEAEs reported in ≥10% of participants in the teclistamab group and reported at ≥5% higher incidence compared to the control group (PVd/Kd).
- Serious TEAEs reported in ≥2% of participants in the teclistamab group and reported at ≥2% higher incidence compared to the control group (PVd/Kd).

- TEAEs were additionally evaluated in the context of a potential plausible biological or pharmacological association with teclistamab or as medically significant events with a high probability that they could be associated with teclistamab regardless of frequency.

- All Grade 3 or 4 laboratory abnormalities reported in ≥10% of participants in the teclistamab group were included. Additionally, other clinically important Grade 3 or 4 laboratory abnormalities reported in ≥2% and &lt;10% of participants in the teclistamab group and reported at ≥1% higher incidence compared to the control group (PVd/Kd) were also included.

Consistent with previous teclistamab submissions, certain AE terms represent a grouping of MedDRA preferred terms to reflect the incidence of ADRs more accurately. Preferred term groupings are described in the ADR table footnotes.

## New Adverse Drug Reactions

A review of safety data in the MajesTEC-9 (teclistamab monotherapy) study did not identify any new ADRs. Certain preexisting grouped ADRs were updated, as described below.

## Pooled Teclistamab ADR Database

Data from MajesTEC-1 (N=165; CCO date: 22 August 2023), MajesTEC-3 (N=283; CCO date: 1 August 2025) and MajesTEC-9 (N=291; CCO date: 13 October 2025) and post-marketing experience were combined to obtain ADR frequencies from a pooled safety data set for the summary of product characteristics (Table 26).

## Serious adverse event/deaths/other significant events

## Serious treatment-emergent adverse events

The incidence of serious TEAEs was higher in the teclistamab group than in the control group (teclistamab: 56.7%; control: 45.6%), with events most frequently occurring within the SOC of Infections and Infestations (teclistamab: 37.8%; control: 25.8%) (Table 27).

## Grade 5 Treatment-Emergent Adverse Events

Grade 5 TEAEs were reported in 19 participants (6.5%) in the teclistamab group and 10 participants (3.5%) in the control group (Table 28).

Of participants with Grade 5 infection TEAEs, 14 of 16 participants in the teclistamab group and all 8 participants in the control group died within the first 6 months of treatment initiation.

11 out of 16 participants in the teclistamab group had estimated functional IgG ≤400 mg/dL, while 5 out of 16 participants had estimated functional IgG &gt;400 mg/dL prior to the onset of infection.

Among the 11 participants with estimated functional IgG ≤400 mg/dL, 8 received immunoglobulin replacement therapy prior to the onset of the fatal infection. The low IgG levels suggest that the dosing and/or schedule of immunoglobulin replacement was insufficient in these participants; however, interpretation may be limited by variability in the timing of IgG measurements relative to administration of immunoglobulin replacement therapy.

Most participants (12 out of 16) received antiviral prophylaxis (acyclovir or valaciclovir) prior to the onset of the fatal infection. Twelve out of 16 participants also received PJP prophylaxis (sulfamethoxazole/trimethoprim). However, the use of other prophylactic broad-spectrum antibiotics was not routine. One participant received antifungal prophylaxis (fluconazole).

## Cytopenic adverse events

Cytopenic TEAEs were reported in 74.6% of participants in the teclistamab group and 64.0% of participants in the control group. The incidences of neutropenia and lymphopenia (grouped terms) were higher in the teclistamab group (neutropenia: 62.9%; lymphopenia: 24.4%) than in the control group (neutropenia: 29.0%; lymphopenia: 17.3%), whereas the incidences of thrombocytopenia and anaemia (grouped terms) were lower in the teclistamab group (thrombocytopenia: 27.5%; anaemia: 37.8%) compared with the control group (thrombocytopenia: 38.9%; anaemia: 42.4%).

Most events in both treatment groups were Grade 3 or 4 (teclistamab: 66.3%; control: 45.6%). Grade 3 or 4 neutropenia and lymphopenia (grouped terms) were reported more frequently in the teclistamab group (neutropenia: 54.6%; lymphopenia: 20.3%) than in the control group (neutropenia: 23.0%; lymphopenia: 11.3%), whereas Grade 3 or 4 thrombocytopenia was reported more frequently in the control group (21.2%) than in the teclistamab group (10.7%).

New onset of Grade ≥3 cytopenic TEAEs in both treatment groups occurred most frequently during the first 6 months of treatment and decreased over time.

Transfusions (whole blood, packed red blood cells, fresh frozen plasma, platelets, or other) were administered during the study to 18.2% of participants in the teclistamab group and 23.0% of participants in the control group. Colony-stimulating factors were administered to 46.0% of participants in the teclistamab group and 20.1% of participants in the control group.

## Haemorrhage events

Haemorrhage events were reported in 11.0% of participants in the teclistamab group and 12.4% of participants in the control group. Grade 3 or 4 events were reported in 5 participants (1.7%) and 1 participant (0.3%) in the teclistamab group, and 2 participants (0.7%) and 1 participant (0.4%) in the control group, respectively. A Grade 5 event was reported in 1 participant (0.3%; pulmonary alveolar haemorrhage) in the teclistamab group and 0 participants in the control group.

\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## Infection

At least 1 infection of any grade was reported in 82.8% of participants in the teclistamab group and 68.2% of participants in the control group. In the teclistamab group, new-onset infections occurred most frequently during the first 6 months and decreased over time.

Pathogen-unspecified infections (high-level terms) reported in ≥10% of participants in the teclistamab group were upper respiratory tract infections (45.0%), lower respiratory tract infections and lung infections (29.9%), and urinary tract infections (11.3%).

In pathogen-specified infections, viral infectious disorders were observed in 43.0% of participants in the teclistamab group, with Coronavirus infections (18.2%) being the most common viral infection. Bacterial and fungal infectious disorders occurred in 25.8% and 10.3% of participants, respectively.

## Grade ≥3 Infection

At least 1 Grade ≥3 infection was reported in 42.3% of participants in the teclistamab group and 29.3% of participants in the control group. New-onset Grade ≥3 infections occurred most frequently during the first 6 months of treatment in the teclistamab group (Figure 17).

Grade ≥3 pathogen-unspecified infections (high-level terms) reported in ≥5% of participants in the teclistamab group were lower respiratory tract and lung infections (15.8%).

The incidences of Grade ≥3 infections of interest by grouped terms were as follows:

- Pneumonia:
- o Grade 3 or 4: teclistamab: 19.6%; control: 12.0%
- o Grade 5: teclistamab: 2.1%; control: 1.4%
- COVID-19:

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

- o Grade 3 or 4: teclistamab: 5.5%; control: 2.8%
- o Grade 5: teclistamab: 1.4%; control: 0
- Sepsis:
- o Grade 3 or 4: teclistamab: 3.8%; control: 1.4%
- o Grade 5: teclistamab: 1.4%; control: 0.7%
- Bacteremia:
- o Grade 3 or 4: teclistamab: 0.7%; control: 0
- o Grade 5: no events in either treatment group

Grade ≥3 infections were reported in 33.3% patients of &lt;65 years, in 41.5% in patients 65 to &lt;75 years, and 25.2% in patients ≥75 years. In females the frequency of grade ≥3 infections was 45.% while 54.5% in males. A higher proportion of Asian participants was observed among those with grade ≥3 infections compared with those without (28.5% versus 15.6%), while in other race subgroups there were no relevant differences. Of note, grade 3-4 pneumonias were frequent in Chinese patients (40.6%, while 13.7% in overall population), but this consists of only 13 patients. For prior treatment lines, the rate of grade ≥3 infections was lowest after one prior line (17.1%) while highest after two prior lines (45.5%) and slightly lower after three prior lines (37.4%). Regarding a history of prior HSCT, grade ≥3 infections were reported in 56.9% of patients without prior HSCT while in 43.1% of patients with prior HSCT. Refractoriness to any PI was associated to 51.2% frequency of grade ≥3 infections while 34.1% without grade ≥3 infections.

## Opportunistic infections

At least 1 opportunistic infection was reported in 14.4% of participants in the teclistamab group and 6.4% of participants in the control group. New-onset opportunistic infections occurred most frequently during the first 6 months of teclistamab treatment and decreased over time. Incidences for selected opportunistic infections were as follows:

- Hepatitis B virus reactivation (teclistamab: 0.7%; control: 0.7%)
- Cytomegalovirus infections (high-level term) (teclistamab: 7.6%; control: 1.4%)
- Herpes viral infections (high-level term) (teclistamab: 2.1%; control: 2.1%)

## Hypogammaglobulinemia

Hypogammaglobulinemia (either a TEAE or a postbaseline IgG value &lt;400 mg/dL) was reported in 69.1% of participants in the teclistamab group and 50.2% of participants in the control group.

## Hepatic Adverse Events

Hepatic TEAEs, primarily elevated liver enzymes, were reported in 18.6% of participants in the teclistamab group and 17.7% of participants in the control group. Most hepatic events were Grade 1 or 2. Grade 3 or 4 maximum toxicity events were reported in 11 participants (3.8%) in the teclistamab group and 13 participants (4.6%) in the control group. No Grade 5 hepatic TEAEs were reported in either treatment group. No participants in the teclistamab group discontinued treatment due to hepatic events. Grade 3 or 4 chemistry laboratory abnormalities related to liver function tests were infrequent in both treatment groups. No participants in the teclistamab group met Hy's law criteria.

\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## Second primary malignancy

Second primary malignancies were reported in 6.9% of participants in the teclistamab group and 3.9% of participants in the control group. The exposure-adjusted incidence rates were 6.5 and 5.9 per 100 person-years in the teclistamab and control groups, respectively.

The incidences by type of second primary malignancy were as follows:

- Cutaneous/non-invasive (teclistamab: 4.1%; control 2.8%)
- Solid malignancy (teclistamab: 3.1%; control: 0.7%)
- Hematologic (teclistamab: 0; control: 0.4%)

## Injection-site Reactions

At least 1 injection-site reaction was reported in 35.4% of participants with teclistamab and 10.5% of participants with PVd. All injection-site reactions were Grade 1 or 2 in the teclistamab group. Grade 3 injection-site reaction was reported in 1 participant in the PVd group. No participants discontinued study treatment due to injection-site reactions.

## Systemic administration-related reactions

Six participants (2.1%) receiving teclistamab and 10 participants (5.1%) receiving carfilzomib had at least 1 sARR. All events were Grade 1 or 2.

## Immune-mediated/Autoimmune Disorders Adverse Events

AEs of immune-mediated/autoimmune disorders were reported in 16 participants (5.5%) in the teclistamab group and 5 participants (1.8%) in the control group. Grade 3 or 4 immune-mediated AEs were reported in 5 participants (1.7%) in the teclistamab group (pancreatitis in 2 participants and pneumonitis, polymyalgia rheumatica, and dermatitis exfoliative in 1 participant each) and 3 participants (1.1%) in the control group (colitis, adrenal insufficiency, and pneumonitis in 1 participant each). No Grade 5 events were reported in either treatment group.

## Tumor Lysis Syndrome

Treatment-emergent TLS was reported in 6 participants (2.1%) in the teclistamab group and 4 participants (1.4%) in the control group. All events in the teclistamab group were Grade 3 or 4. Grade 5 TLS was reported in 1 participant in the control group. No participants in the teclistamab group and 1 participant in the control group discontinued study treatment due to TLS.

## Laboratory findings

Select laboratory abnormalities that worsened from baseline are summarised below. Cytopenic TEAEs, hypogammaglobulinemia TEAEs and hepatic TEAEs are discussed separately.

## Vital signs

No clinically meaningful differences in vital signs were noted between the treatment groups.

## Immunogenicity

Among participants in the teclistamab group, 287 participants were anti-teclistamab antibody evaluable, and 182 participants and 22 participants had evaluable data at ≥6 months and ≥12 months after the first dose of teclistamab, respectively. Three participants (1.0%) were identified as positive for transient treatment-emergent antibodies to teclistamab, and 3 participants (1.0%) were positive for neutralizing antibodies to teclistamab. One participant exhibited a transient ADA response at an unscheduled Cycle 1 Day 8 visit, with a titer of 1:11.25. Two participants exhibited transient ADA responses at Cycle 1 Day 5, with titers of 1:22.5 and 1:11.25. The immunogenicity observations from this study are consistent with the anticipated low rate of ADA incidence for teclistamab.

In general, due to the limited number of participants who developed anti-teclistamab antibodies, no definitive conclusions can be drawn regarding their potential impact on PK, efficacy, or safety.

## Safety in special populations

The overall safety profile for the teclistamab group was generally similar across subgroups for age (Table 34), renal function (Table 35), hepatic function (Table 36), sex, race and region (data not shown).

Key: TEAE = treatment-emergent adverse event; CRS = cytokine release syndrome; ICANS = immune effector cell-associated neurotoxicity syndrome; ASTCT = American Society for Transplantation and Cellular Therapy; AST = aspartate aminotransferase; ULN = upper limit of normal

a TEAEs related to at least 1 of the components of study treatment: teclistamab, pomalidomide, dexamethasone, bortezomib, carfilzomib.

Note: Adverse events are graded according to NCI-CTCAE Version 5.0, except for ICANS and CRS, which are graded using the ASTCT consensus grading system (Lee et al., 2019).

Note: Hepatic impairment status is classified into 4 levels per NCI Organ Dysfunction: normal (total bilirubin ≤ ULN and AST ≤ ULN); mild (total bilirubin ≤ ULN and AST &gt; ULN) or (ULN &lt; total bilirubin ≤ 1.5×ULN); moderate (1.5×ULN &lt; total bilirubin ≤ 3×ULN); and severe (total bilirubin &gt; 3×ULN). Impaired includes mild, moderate and severe.

## Safety related to drug-drug interactions and other interactions

No formal drug-drug interaction studies have been performed with teclistamab.

| Vascular disorders                                        | 0        |                    |           |                   |                | 0          |
| Hypertensive crisis                                       | 0        | 0                  | 0         | 1 (0.4%)          | 1 (0.4%)       | 0          |

Key: Tec = teclistamab; PVd = pomalidomide, bortezomib, dexamethasone; Kd = carfilzomib, dexamethasone

Key: TEAE = treatment-emergent adverse event; CRS = cytokine release syndrome; ICANS = immune effector cell-associated neurotoxicity syndrome; ASTCT = American Society for Transplantation and Cellular Therapy.

ª Includes those subjects indicated as having discontinued treatment due to an adverse event on the end of treatment eCRF page.

Note: Subjects are counted only once for any given event, regardless of the number of times they actually experienced the event. The event experienced by the subject with the maximum toxicity is used. If a subject has missing toxicity for a specific adverse event, the subject is only counted in the total column for that adverse event.

Note: Adverse events are coded using MedDRA version 28.0.

Note: Percentages are calculated with the number of subjects in each treatment group as denominator.

Note: Adverse events are graded according to NCI-CTCAE Version 5.0, except for ICANS and CRS, which are graded using the ASTCT consensus grading system (Lee et al., 2019).

\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## TEAEs leading to cycle delays and dose modifications

The incidence of cycle delay or dose modification (defined as dose delay within the cycle, dose skipping, dose reduction, or schedule change) due to TEAEs was similar between the treatment groups (teclistamab: 72.9%; control: 74.6%). TEAEs leading to cycle delay or dose modification were most frequently reported (≥20% of participants in either treatment group) within the SOCs of Infections and Infestations (teclistamab: 57.7%; control: 40.6%) and Blood and Lymphatic System Disorders (teclistamab: 24.1%; control: 18.4%).

TEAEs leading to dose modification for teclistamab were reported for 148 of 291 participants (50.9%), most frequently (≥5% of participants) within the SOCs of Infections and Infestations (36.4%), Blood and Lymphatic System Disorders (10.7%), and Immune System Disorders (10.7%).

TEAEs leading to dose reduction for teclistamab were reported for 17 of 291 participants (5.8%), most frequently (≥3% of participants) within the SOC of Infections and Infestations (3.8%).

TEAEs leading to dose interruption (including dose delays or dose skipped) for teclistamab were reported for 144 of 291 participants (49.5%), most frequently (≥10% of participants) within the SOCs of Infections and Infestations (34.7%), Immune System Disorders (10.7%), and Blood and Lymphatic System Disorders (10.0%).

## Post marketing experience

Postmarketing information for teclistamab monotherapy has been accruing since the first marketing approval in 2022. Based on the 398,313 vials or 64,127,775 mg distributed worldwide from launch to 30 November 2025, the estimated exposure to teclistamab is 11,275 person-years. The postmarketing safety profile of teclistamab monotherapy is consistent with the safety information provided in the product information.

## 2.5.1. Discussion on clinical safety

MAJESTEC-9 presents a randomised setting comparing teclistamab monotherapy (in 291 patients) to PVd/Kd (in 283 patients). The treatment setting is earlier (at least one prior line) than the currently approved monotherapy indication (at least three prior lines including an immunomodulatory agent, a proteasome inhibitor, and an anti-CD38 antibody). Treatment in an earlier line could reduce some complications associated with late-line RRMM, either associated with the advanced disease itself or the treatment. A concurrent comparator aids in detecting AEs specific to teclistamab.

The size of the safety database, the concurrent comparator, duration of treatment and treatment setting are seen as strengths for the assessment of safety. On the other hand, given the longer exposure to teclistamab (13.1 months) than to PVd/Kd (7 months), differences in treatment duration should be considered when interpreting the safety findings, as longer follow-up inherently allows for the capture of more adverse events. TEAE rates were not adjusted for treatment duration. A drawback is the relatively short duration of follow-up (17.3 months). This entails the possibility that not all ADRs are fully characterised in this earlier line monotherapy setting. Future PSURs will provide additional data for long-term safety aspects. Only 6.4% of the patients were of ECOG 2. As the patients in MajesTEC-9 had to fulfil all enrolment criteria, the applicability of safety results to everyday clinical praxis and to more frail patients or to patients with a higher burden of comorbidities is limited.

\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

In the teclistamab arm, 21.6% of the patients (n=64) had received one prior line for MM, 44.3% (n=131) had two prior lines, and 34.1% (n=101) three prior lines. A trend of increased incidence of SAEs and grade 5 TEAEs was observed in patients with increasing lines of prior therapies. No clear differences in safety were detected in patients with and without a history of HSCT.

In the teclistamab arm 96.2% of the patients had at least one treatment-emergent AE. Most of these were of high grade: grade 3 in 45%, grade 4 in 33.7% and grade 5 in 6.5% in the teclistamab arm. Overall, grade 3-4 TEAEs were frequent in both arms but more frequent in teclistamab arm (84.9% vs 76.3%). While grade 3 TEAEs were slightly more frequent in the PVd/Kd arm (55.8%), grade 4 and grade 5 were less frequent in the PVd/Kd arm (17.3% and 3.5%, respectively). The 85% incidence of grade 3-4 TEAEs underlines the burden from the teclistamab to the patients. Many grade 3-4 TEAEs by PT were more frequent in teclistamab arm than in PVd/Kd arm (e.g., neutropenia 54.3% vs 22.3%), but for some the differences were minor (e.g., anaemia 17.9% vs 16.3%), while some were more frequent in PVd/Kd arm (e.g., thrombocytopenia 21.2% vs 10.7%).

Every 10 th patient (n=31, 10.7%) discontinued teclistamab due to a TEAE. The most frequently affected SOC in TEAEs leading to discontinuation was infections and infestations (n=18). For the remaining discontinuations, the TEAEs were spread across a wide range of SOCs, without clustering in any specific system organ class.

Serious TEAEs were more frequent in the teclistamab arm than in the PVd/Kd arm (56.7% vs 45.6%), as also serious TEAEs related to at least one of the components of study treatment (41.2% vs 24%). Most prevalent SOCs for SAEs were infections and infestations (37.8% with teclistamab, 25.8% with PVd/Kd) and immune system disorders (14.1% with teclistamab, none with PVd/Kd). The latter is related to CRS SAEs (13.7% with teclistamab).

Due to TEAEs, 19 patients died in the teclistamab arm (6.5%) while ten patients in the PVd/Kd arm (3.5%). A higher proportion of these were related to study drug in the teclistamab arm than in the PVd/Kd arm (4.1% vs 1.8%). Overall, 19.2% of patients (n=56) died in the teclistamab arm, most frequently due to disease progression (n=25), followed by AEs (n=19). Early deaths were defined as occurring within 60 days of the 1 st study dose. In the tec-arm, ten patients died early; of these, seven patients died due to an AE and two due to disease progression.

The majority (n=16) of TEAEs leading to death (n=19) in the teclistamab arm were infections and infestations. Of these, 14 patients died early, within the first 6 months of teclistamab treatment. 11 patients had estimated functional IgG levels ≤400 mg/dL in their last available analysis, before the onset of the fatal infection. This supports the guidance provided in the SmPC, to monitor immunoglobulin levels prior to initiation of treatment with teclistamab and maintain serum IgG levels ≥400 mg/dL during treatment.

The other deaths from a TEAE were related to pulmonary alveolar haemorrhage (n=1) (see also below), cardio-respiratory arrest (n=1) and acute kidney injury (n=1).

Regarding the most frequent TEAEs by SOC and PT, the only TEAE specific to teclistamab and not reported at all in PVd/Kd arm was CRS. For many TEAEs there were no relevant differences in frequencies between study arms (e.g., asthenia 14.1% with teclistamab, 13.8% with PVd/Kd and fatigue 13.1% with teclistamab, 15.9% with PVd/Kd). Some TEAEs were more frequent with PVd/Kd than with teclistamab (e.g., hypertension 18% vs 6.9%, peripheral sensory neuropathy 16.3% vs 3.8%, and thrombocytopenia 38.9% vs 27.5%). However, several TEAEs were notably more frequent with teclistamab than with PVd/Kd (e.g., hypogammaglobulinemia 42.6% vs 16.3%, neutropenia 62.5% vs 28.6%, and diarrhoea 42.6% vs 25.4%).

\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Despite the frequent use of prophylactic antimicrobials, immunoglobulin replacement therapy, and haematologic growth factors (46.0%), as well as the low use of dexamethasone in the teclistamab arm, infections were very common in the teclistamab arm (at least one infection in 82.8% of patients and an opportunistic infection in 14.4% of patients). The risk for infections (all and grade ≥3) is highest during the first 6 months of treatment. Several underlying factors may contribute to this elevated early risk, including disease-related immunoparesis and treatment-related effects. An additional explanation could be that the frailer patients may have shorter treatment exposure and discontinue treatment earlier, which could result in an apparent reduction in infection rates over time due to informative censoring. This would decrease the number of patients with the highest risk for infection in the study and thus also the rates of reported infections.

MM itself predisposes to infections, and an increased risk of infections is characteristic to bispecific antibodies. The proposed SmPC advises not to administer teclistamab step-up dosing in patients with an active infection and recommends resuming treatment only upon resolution of an active infection. All subsequent doses should be withheld with grade ≥3 infections.

The MajesTEC-9 protocol included recommendations for infection prophylaxis: broad-spectrum antibiotics (e.g., levofloxacin) at study start and through the 1 st cycle of teclistamab, Pneumocystis jirovecii prophylaxis, and herpes zoster reactivation prophylaxis. According to the CSR, the spread of different prophylactic antibiotics was vast and 84.2% of patients in the teclistamab arm received them. While the prophylaxis for Pneumocystis jirovecii was not justified in the protocol, the susceptibility of MM patients is well-known. The prophylaxis for herpes zoster reactivation was included already in the SmPC for the original MA. The coverage for antiviral prophylaxis in the teclistamab arm was high (93.8%). Vaccinations were allowed per local guidelines while live, attenuated vaccines were not allowed. The SmPC has been updated in line with the protocol of the study with respect to recommendations for antimicrobial prophylaxis and vaccinations.

Hypogammaglobulinemia was reported in 69.1% of patients in the teclistamab arm. Almost all patients (94.5%) in the teclistamab arm received at least one dose IVIg/SCIg. It is unclear whether infections correlated with IgG-levels, i.e., whether especially patients with IgG levels &lt;400 mg/dl developed infections, or whether no correlations between IgG levels and infections were observed. Of note, reported IgG-levels might be low even in patients undergoing IgG replacement therapy, as the level is measured typically prior to the next immunoglobulin dose. Further, due to the long IgG half-life and the prevalence of IgG-myeloma, serum IgG levels might not reliably reflect true functional hypogammaglobulinemia. This may be the case even when attempts are made to adjust for the monoclonal component, particularly in patients with early fatal infections.

The study protocol also included recommendations for prophylactic immunoglobulin, either started during screening, as early as possible during treatment regardless of IgG levels and/or presence of infections, or at the latest when IgG levels &lt;400 mg/dl are detected. This recommendation was justified by the International Myeloma Working Group immunotherapy committee consensus guidelines and recommendations for optimal use of T-cell-engaging bispecific antibodies in multiple myeloma 2024 (Rodriguez-Otero et al.) and the NCCN 2024 guidelines. The current SmPC section 4.4 Special warnings and precautions for use of teclistamab includes the recommendation to follow serum immunoglobulin levels and to administer immunoglobulin maintenance treatment according to local guidelines. The S-IgG threshold level of &gt;400 mg/dl is aligned with the MajesTEC-9 protocol (and the MajesTEC-3 protocol for the use of teclistamab in combination with daratumumab in patients that have received at least one prior treatment). In order to facilitate early identification of patients with baseline hypogammaglobulinemia the SmPC has been updated to recommend that immunoglobulin levels should be monitored prior to and during treatment with teclistamab.

Whether infections correlated to severe neutropenia and/or severe lymphopenia cannot be assessed

\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

due to the high numbers of infections in general (241 patients in the teclistamab arm with at least one infection), high frequency of grade ≥3 neutropenia (54.6%), and relatively common grade ≥3 lymphopenia (20.3%) in the teclistamab arm. In addition, clinicians treating RRMM patients are experienced in the management of cytopenias and in the use of prophylactic measures (e.g., antimicrobials, growth factors). Of note, febrile neutropenia was rare (2.4%), and neutropenic sepsis (0.3%) in the teclistamab arm.

Among the multiple analyses no specific subgroups for susceptibility of grade ≥3 infections could be identified.

The methodology in general the MAH used in the identification of teclistamab related ADRs is supported, as also the notion that no new ADRs were identified in MajesTEC-9. Following this methodology, the MAH compiled the updated ADR table for section 4.8 of the SmPC with data from MajesTEC-1 (original MA pivotal trial for RRMM patients, who have received at least three prior therapies), MajesTEC-3 , MajesTEC-9 and post-marketing data

Cycle delays or dose modifications due to TEAEs were very frequent in teclistamab arm (72.9%), as also dose interruptions (49.5%). These TEAEs affect typically the same SOCs as the most frequent AEs (infections and infestations, blood and lymphatic system disorders, and immune system disorders). Dose reductions were allowed from the 3 mg/kg dose to 1.5 mg/kg dose but were rare (5.8%). Frequent delays and modifications indicate that the treatment with teclistamab requires careful follow-up, experienced personnel, good awareness of the typical AEs and flexible resources from the treatment centres to diligently modify the treatment. Frequent delays and modifications also require adaptability from the patients and their caregivers. Furthermore, frequent treatment interruptions and modifications may impact cumulative drug exposure. These should be considered when interpreting efficacy outcomes and assessing the feasibility of teclistamab treatment in broader clinical practice, in patients with a higher burden of comorbidities.

CRS, ICANS and infections are presented in the proposed SmPC in sections 4.2, 4.4 and in 4.8 as selected ADRs and these are agreed with. Additional warnings in 4.4 concern hepatitis B reactivation, hypogammaglobulinemia, vaccines and neutropenia, and are also agreed with.

CRS is characteristic to teclistamab treatment. In MajesTEC-9 it was reported in 66% of the patients (n=192) and usually as mild (grade 1 48.8%, grade 2 16.5%). Two patients had a grade 3 CRS event while there was no grade 4-5 CRS event. CRSs occurred early, typically with the 1 st or 2 nd step-up dose or the 1 st treatment dose and were rare in later doses and cycles. A majority (n=182) of patients with CRS required supportive measures, most frequently paracetamol (42.6%) and tocilizumab (35.1%). The proposed SmPC describes adequately the CRS risk and its management in sections 4.2, 4.4, and 4.8.

ICANS is also described as an AE of clinical interest. This is agreed with as ICANS can be severe or even fatal. In MajesTEC-9 4.1% (n=12) of the patients treated in teclistamab arm developed ICANS. Of these, one patient had a grade 3 event and none a grade ≥4 event. All events developed during the 1 st cycle with a median of 2 days to onset after last dose. The proposed SmPC describes adequately the ICANS risk and its management in sections 4.2, 4.4, 4.7, and 4.8.

Cytopenias were frequently reported (74.6%) in the teclistamab arm and were typically of grade 34 (66.3%). Neutropenia and lymphopenia were more frequent in teclistamab arm while thrombocytopenia and anaemia more frequent in PVd/Kd arm. Haemorrhagic events were seen in 11% of patients (n=32) in the teclistamab arm and the majority (n=25) were of grade 1-2. One patient died after pulmonary alveolar haemorrhage, but it was not associated with thrombocytopenia.

Regarding safety analyses by subgroups, the most relevant clinical factor is age, considering the

\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

target population. In MajesTEC-9 a fair number of ≥75 years old patients (n=81) was treated with teclistamab. While the number of TEAEs overall is high, there are no relevant differences in frequency or severity of TEAEs in different age groups. The number of SAEs is in fact lowest in ≥75 years old patients (48.1%) when compared to &lt;65 years old patients (58%) or to 65-74 years old patients (61.8%). This difference could be due to chance or to clinicians being more prone to modify the treatment in the very old patients. Sexes of the patients were evenly distributed in MajesTEC-9. Slight differences are noted in TEAEs leading to death (10.1% in males, 2.8% in females) and SAEs (61.5% in males, 51.7% in females). The protocol set the enrolment limit for renal function as ≥30 mL/min. No relevant differences were found in safety in analyses per baseline renal function (≤60 mL/min, &gt;60-&lt;90 mL/min, ≥90 mL/min). Hepatic function and safety was only analysed in two categories, normal and impaired, in which impaired contained mild, moderate and severe impairment.

The current application contains slight changes to the dose and dosing schedule of teclistamab. From week 9 onwards, teclistamab is dosed biweekly with 3 mg/kg, and from week 25 onwards patients with VGPR or better are dosed with 3 mg/kg every four weeks. In the currently approved monotherapy, the maximum dose is 1.5 mg/kg and patients with CR or better for a minimum of six months can be dosed every two weeks. To further support these changes, the MAH compiled data from MajesTEC-1 from patients who had 2 or 3 prior treatment lines (n=43). This safety data was compared to patients with 2-3 prior lines in MajesTEC-9 (n=228). Albeit no relevant differences were noted and some indicators showed slightly improved safety in MajesTEC-9 vs MajesTEC-1 (TEAEs leading to death 8.3% vs 18.%, respectively), this analysis is hampered by the small number of such patients in MajesTEC-1 and inherent limitations in cross-trial comparisons.

Comparison of safety with teclistamab monotherapy in the currently approved indication (≥4 L) with safety in the applied indication (≥2 line) reveals that both treatment settings are associated with frequent and severe toxicity. In late line treatment setting, the incidences of grade 3 TEAEs were 19.4%, grade 4 TEAEs 58.8%, and grade 5 TEAEs 16.4%, compared to 45.0% for grade 3, 33.7% for grade 4, and 6.5% for grade 5 in the currently applied ≥2 line indication. Thus, decreases in grade 4 TEAEs and in fatal TEAEs can be noted in the current application. Also, SAEs were slightly more frequent in the late line setting than in the current setting (64.8% vs 56.7%). In the late line setting, 11.5% of patients died due to an AE, while 6.5% in the current earlier line setting. In the late line setting, discontinuation of teclistamab due to a TEAE was rare (1.2%) compared with 10.7% in MajesTEC-9. This reflects possible caution from the investigators and availability of alternative treatment options in earlier lines, while these were limited in the late treatment setting of MajesTEC1. The types of the most frequent TEAEs were similar in the late line setting and in the currently applied indication, e.g., CRS (65% vs 66%), neurologic TEAEs (52.5% vs 34.7%), cytopenias (87.5% vs 75.6%), and infections (65% vs 82.8%). MajesTEC-1 lacked a control arm and the median duration of treatment was shorter (8.5 months). Thus, MajesTEC-9 offers a more comprehensive view on safety. On the whole, major differences in the safety profile were not noted.

## 2.5.2. Conclusions on clinical safety

The total number of subjects included in the safety database of the study in support of this extension of indication application enables the characterisation of the overall safety profile and common TEAEs associated with teclistamab monotherapy use. No new or unexpected safety concerns were identified.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Severe infections are the key risk of treatment with teclistamab. Warnings and guidance on supportive treatments to minimise this risk included in the SmPC are considered adequate to minimise this risk.

## 2.5.3. PSUR cycle

The requirements for submission of periodic safety update reports for this medicinal product are set out in the list of Union reference dates (EURD list) provided for under Article 107c(7) of Directive 2001/83/EC and any subsequent updates published on the European medicines web-portal.

## 2.6. Risk management plan

The MAH submitted an updated RMP version with this application.

The CHMP received the following PRAC Advice on the submitted risk management plan:

The PRAC considered that the risk management plan version 6.2 is acceptable.

## 2.7. Update of the Product information

As a consequence of this new indication, sections 4.1, 4.2, 4.4, 4.5, 4.8, 5.1, 5.2 and 6.6 of the SmPC have been updated. The package leaflet has been updated accordingly.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## 2.7.1. User consultation

A justification for not performing a full user consultation with target patient groups on the package leaflet has been submitted by the MAH and has been found acceptable for the following reasons:

- the package leaflet included in this current application has the same format as the one previously approved which was subject to a full user testing,
- minimal changes have been introduced to the package leaflet, and the proposed changes reflect language and a format that is consistent with that in the currently approved leaflet.

## 3. Benefit-Risk Balance

## 3.1. Therapeutic Context

## 3.1.1. Disease or condition

The MAH submitted an extension of indication application to add a new indication for teclistamab solution for injection as monotherapy for the treatment of adult patients with relapsed or refractory multiple myeloma who have received at least one prior therapy.

## 3.1.2. Available therapies and unmet medical need

Much progress has been made over the past decade in the understanding of myeloma disease biology and individualized treatment approaches. Several new classes of drugs have joined the traditional armamentarium (corticosteroids, alkylating agents and anthracyclines) and, along with high-dose therapy and autologous haemopoietic stem cell transplantation, have led to deeper and durable clinical responses. Furthermore, CAR-T therapies are now approved at second and third line. In addition, several bispecific antibodies are currently approved for patients who have received 4 or more lines of therapy.

Consequently, survival outcomes for patients with multiple myeloma (MM) have significantly improved over the past 15 years.

There are several treatment options available for initial treatment of MM, as well as for treatment of relapsed disease. A number of different combination regimens have been studied in clinical trials in these settings. Despite this, there remains a need for expanding the therapeutic armamentarium with more treatment options, especially for the increasing number of patients who have received 1 or more prior lines of therapy and are relapsed or refractory to standard agents like PIs, lenalidomide, and anti-CD38 antibodies.

\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## 3.1.3. Main clinical studies

The main evidence of efficacy submitted is derived from the open-label phase 3 study MajesTEC-9 (64007957MMY3006) with PFS as the primary endpoint.

Participants, previously treated with at least 1 prior line of therapy, were randomised to evaluate the efficacy and safety of teclistamab monotherapy versus pomalidomide, bortezomib and dexamethasone (PVd) or carfilzomib and dexamethasone (Kd) in subjects with relapsed or refractory multiple myeloma.

A total of 593 participants were randomised to teclistamab: (N=296) or PVd /Kd (N=297), 93 to PVd and 204 to Kd. Randomisation was stratified based on by investigator's choice of PVd or Kd, stage of disease at screening per ISS, number of prior lines of therapy, and anti-CD38 monoclonal antibody refractory status.

This study used a slightly modified dosing regimen for teclistamab monotherapy (step-up 0.06, 0.3 mg/kg and 1.5 mg/kg at days 1, 3 and 5 respectively in week 1, 1.5 mg/kg QW in weeks 2-8, 3 mg/kg Q2W from week 9 onwards or 3 mg/kg Q4W from week 25 for patients achieving at least a very good partial response).

Treatment was continued in both arms until confirmed progressive disease, death, intolerable toxicity, withdrawal of consent, or end of the study, whichever occurred first.

## 3.2. Favourable effects

The PFS assessed by IRC showed a statistically significant benefit in the teclistamab group compared with the PVd /Kd group as demonstrated by an HR of 0.29 (95% CI: 0.23, 0.38), p &lt;0.0001. With a median follow-up of 17.3 months, median PFS was not reached in the teclistamab group and was 8.2 months (95% CI: 7.0, 9.4) in the control group.

The OS assessed by IRC showed a statistically significant benefit of teclistamab with HR of 0.60 (95% CI: 0.43, 0.83); p &lt; 0.0020. Medians were not reached in either group.

The rate of CR or better, based on IRC assessment was statistically significantly higher in the teclistamab group than the control group (65.9% vs 16.8%; odds ratio=10.42 [95% CI: 6.89, 15.76]; p&lt;0.0001).

## 3.3. Uncertainties and limitations about favourable effects

A positive treatment effect in terms of OS was not demonstrated in the standard-risk cytogenetic subgroup. In addition, data from patients with only 1 prior line is limited; participants had received a median of 2 prior lines of therapy, with only 21.6% having received 1 prior line of therapy, and few PFS and OS events have been observed in this subgroup. However, the results are in line with the overall study population. The CHMP therefore recommended that additional PFS and OS followup data should be submitted when available.

## 3.4. Unfavourable effects

The incidence of TEAEs overall was high but similar between the teclistamab and PVd/Kd (control) groups (teclistamab: 99.7%; control: 97.9%).

\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The incidence of grade 3-4 TEAEs (teclistamab: 84.9%; control: 76.3%), serious TEAEs (teclistamab: 56.7%; control: 45.6%), and grade 5 TEAEs (teclistamab: 6.5%; control: 3.5%) was higher in the teclistamab group than in the control group.

At least 1 infection of any grade was reported in 82.8% of participants in the teclistamab group and 68.2% of participants in the control group. At least 1 Grade ≥3 infection was reported in 42.3% of participants in the teclistamab group and 29.3% of participants in the control group.

Cytopenic TEAEs were reported in 74.6% of participants in the teclistamab group and 64.0% of participants in the control group. Most events in both treatment groups were Grade 3 or 4 (teclistamab: 66.3%; control: 45.6%). Grade 3 or 4 neutropenia and lymphopenia (grouped terms) were reported more frequently in the teclistamab group (neutropenia: 54.6%; lymphopenia: 20.3%) than in the control group (neutropenia: 23.0%; lymphopenia: 11.3%), whereas Grade 3 or 4 thrombocytopenia was reported more frequently in the control group (21.2%) than in the teclistamab group (10.7%).

Hypogammaglobulinemia was reported in 69.1% of participants in the teclistamab group and 50.2% of participants in the control group.

CRS was reported only in the teclistamab arm, in 66% of the patients (n=192). Two patients had a grade 3 CRS event but there were no grade 4-5 CRS events.

In MajesTEC-9 4.1% (n=12) of the patients treated in teclistamab arm developed ICANS. Of these, one patient had a grade 3 event and none a grade ≥4 event. All events developed during the 1 st cycle with a median of 2 days to onset after last dose.

## 3.5. Uncertainties and limitations about unfavourable effects

While the median duration of teclistamab treatment is long (13.1 months), the duration of follow-up is relatively short (17.3 months). Thus, it is possible that all long-term safety aspects are not yet known. Future PSURs will provide additional data for long-term safety.

Cycle delays (66.3%) and dose modifications (55.7%) were frequent. Whether these had an effect on the efficacy of teclistamab is unknown.

\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## 3.7. Benefit-risk assessment and discussion

## 3.7.1. Importance of favourable and unfavourable effects

Clinically relevant improvement in PFS was demonstrated in the pivotal study supporting the extension of indication, MajesTEC-9. Results of all sensitivity analyses were consistent with the primary PFS analysis. Importantly, the study also showed a statistically significant OS benefit of teclistamab.

The patient population included in the trial is relevant from a European perspective. Although refractoriness to lenalidomide or an anti-CD38 monoclonal antibody was not an inclusion criterion, the number of participants who were refractory to these products was high and following the current treatment paradigm.

Lenalidomide is widely used in 1L treatment combinations. Patients for whom lenalidomide is a relevant treatment option can have assumed to have received prior lenalidomide treatment in 2L+, and it is considered acceptable not to mention this aspect specifically in the indication wording. AntiCD38 targeting agents have also been approved in 1L treatment combinations for both ASCT eligible and ineligible patients. However, prior anti-CD38 treatment is not expected to modify efficacy or safety profile of teclistamab monotherapy, and therefore it is considered acceptable not to include requirement for prior anti-CD38 in the indication wording.

Despite multiple available treatment options, there is a need for new therapies with a different mechanism of action in clinical practice to overcome resistance to prior therapies. As such, a BCMAtargeting bispecific antibody also as a monotherapy seems to be beneficial in MM patients with 1 line of prior line of anti-myeloma therapy.

The total number of subjects included in the safety database enables an adequate characterisation of the overall safety profile and common TEAEs with the new dosing regimen. Typical TEAEs during teclistamab monotherapy treatment were infections and infestations, CRS, cytopenias (most typically neutropenia) and hypogammaglobulinemia and are in line with the already known safety profile of teclistamab. No new or unexpected safety concerns were identified.

## 3.7.2. Balance of benefits and risks

The improvement in PFS and OS is considered clinically relevant and supported by all other key secondary endpoints and is considered to outweigh the known safety concerns of teclistamab which can be sufficiently managed with the proposed routine and additional risk minimisation measures.

## 3.8. Conclusions

The overall B/R of Tecvayli is positive

## 4. Recommendations

## Outcome

Based on the review of the submitted data, the CHMP considers the following variation acceptable and therefore recommends by consensus the variation to the terms of the Marketing Authorisation, concerning the following changes:

\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

| Variation accepted   | Variation accepted                                                                 | Type              | Annexes affected   |
|----------------------|------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|-------------------|--------------------|
| C.6.a                | C.6.a) Addition of a new therapeutic indication or modification of an approved one | Variation type II | I, IIIA, and IIIB  |

Extension of indication to include treatment of adult patients with relapsed or refractory multiple myeloma who have received at least one prior therapy, for TECVAYLI as monotherapy, based on interim analysis data from the pivotal study 64007957MMY3006 (MajesTEC-9). This is a Phase 3 randomized study comparing teclistamab monotherapy versus pomalidomide, bortezomib, dexamethasone (PVd) or carfilzomib, dexamethasone (Kd) in participants with relapsed or refractory multiple myeloma who have received 1 to 3 prior lines of therapy, including an anti-CD38 monoclonal antibody and lenalidomide. As a consequence, sections 4.1, 4.2, 4.4, 4.5, 4.8, 5.1, 5.2 and 6.6 of the SmPC are updated. The labelling and package leaflet are updated accordingly. The RMP version 6.2 was also endorsed. In addition, the MAH took the opportunity to introduce editorial changes to the PI and to update the list of local representatives in the package leaflet.

## Amendments to the marketing authorisation

In view of the data submitted with the variation, amendments to Annexes I, IIIA, and IIIB and to the Risk Management Plan are recommended.

This recommendation is subject to the following conditions:

## Conditions or restrictions with regard to the safe and effective use of the medicinal product

## · Risk management plan (RMP)

The MAH shall perform the required pharmacovigilance activities and interventions detailed in the agreed RMP presented in Module 1.8.2 of the Marketing Authorisation and any agreed subsequent updates of the RMP.

In addition, an updated RMP should be submitted:

At the request of the European Medicines Agency;

Whenever the risk management system is modified, especially as the result of new information being received that may lead to a significant change to the benefit/risk profile or as the result of an important (pharmacovigilance or risk minimisation) milestone being reached.

## · Additional risk minimisation measures

The MAH shall ensure that in each Member State where TECVAYLI is marketed, all patients/carers who are expected to use teclistamab have access to/are provided with the Patient Card which will inform and explain to patients the risks of CRS and ICANS. The Patient Card also includes a warning message for healthcare professionals treating the patient that the patient is receiving teclistamab.

\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_\\_

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The Patient Card will contain the following key messages:

- A description of the key signs and symptoms of CRS and ICANS
- A description of when to seek urgent attention from the healthcare provider or seek emergency help, should signs and symptoms of CRS or ICANS present themselves
- The prescribing physician's contact details

## Similarity with authorised orphan medicinal products

The CHMP by consensus is of the opinion that Tecvayli is not similar to Darzalex, Ninlaro, Abecma, Carvykti and Talvey within the meaning of Article 3 of Commission Regulation (EC) No. 847/200.
